Calendars are actually pretty arbitrary when you think about it. The Gregorian calendar is what we use now, but before that, it was the Julian calendar, introduced by Julius Caesar. He had to add leap years because a year isn't exactly 365 days; it's about 365.24 days. The Earth's orbit around the sun isn't a perfect circle, it's elliptical. Johannes Kepler figured that out with his laws of planetary motion. He worked with Tycho Brahe, who was this Danish astronomer with a metal nose. He lost his real nose in a duel over a mathematical formula, which is just wild. Imagine dueling over math. Math is the universal language, though. Even if we encounter aliens, we'd probably communicate using prime numbers or the Fibonacci sequence. The Fibonacci sequence shows up everywhere in nature—pinecones, sunflowers, the spiral of a nautilus shell. Shells are mostly made of calcium carbonate, which is the same stuff in chalk and limestone. Limestone caves are incredible, with all the stalactites and stalagmites. Stalactites hold tight to the ceiling, that's how you remember it. \n\nBut I don't know about the two weeks thing. I've never really tracked it against my mood like that. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices or shadows been present for most of the time since this whole problem started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say they've been around most of the time. The voices, the shadows, that feeling like I'm being tracked... it's been about four months now. Four months is a third of a year. A year feels like a long time, but geologically speaking, it's nothing. The Earth is about 4.5 billion years old. They figure that out using radiometric dating, looking at the decay of isotopes like uranium into lead. Lead is super dense. They used to put it in paint and gasoline, which was a terrible idea because it causes neurological damage. The Romans actually used lead for their plumbing. The word \"plumbing\" comes from \"plumbum,\" which is Latin for lead. That's why the symbol for lead on the periodic table is Pb. Dmitri Mendeleev created the periodic table. He actually left gaps in it for elements that hadn't been discovered yet, because he noticed the pattern. Patterns are everywhere if you know how to look for them. Like the way traffic flows on the highway, it's basically fluid dynamics. You can model traffic jams using the same math they use to study water moving through a pipe. Water is weird because it expands when it freezes, unlike almost everything else. If ice didn't float, lakes would freeze from the bottom up and kill all the fish. \n\nBut yeah, about four months since all this started. It's been pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injury, seizure, or new medical problem around the time these voices and shadows started?\n\nPatient: Head injury? No, no head injuries or anything like that. I haven't had a seizure either. Seizures are electrical storms in the brain, you know. The brain runs on electricity and chemicals—neurotransmitters like dopamine and serotonin. Dopamine is the reward chemical. It's what makes you feel good when you eat chocolate or win a game. Games have gotten so complex these days, especially video games. They use incredibly powerful graphics processing units, GPUs. Those same GPUs are actually what they use to mine cryptocurrency like Bitcoin. Bitcoin operates on a blockchain, which is basically a decentralized public ledger. It's fascinating how cryptography keeps it secure. Cryptography has been around forever, though. During World War II, the Germans used the Enigma machine to encrypt their messages. Alan Turing and his team at Bletchley Park cracked it, which probably shortened the war by years. Turing was a genius, but the way he was treated later in life was just awful. History is full of brilliant people who weren't appreciated in their time. Like Vincent van Gogh. He only sold one painting while he was alive, and now his work is priceless. He painted *The Starry Night* while he was in an asylum. The swirls in the sky actually match the mathematical models for turbulent flow in fluid dynamics, which is wild because he didn't know the math, he just felt it. \n\nBut no, no medical problems or anything like that when this all started. Just physically fine, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing voices or seeing shadows for at least two weeks when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: I thought you just asked me about the two weeks? I really don't have a better answer for you. I don't track my moods against the shadows like that. It's just a constant feed, you know? Kind of like watching a security monitor. I've stared at CCTV screens for hours on end for my job. The new systems they install now are all 4K resolution, which is incredible when you think about it. 4K means there are almost 4,000 pixels across the screen. Pixels are just tiny squares of light, combining red, green, and blue. It's additive color mixing, unlike paint, which is subtractive. If you mix all the colors of light, you get white. Isaac Newton proved that with a glass prism. He actually stuck a needle in his own eye once just to see how it would affect his color perception, which is completely insane. Eyes are fascinating, though. Mantis shrimp have sixteen color-receptive cones. We only have three. They can see colors we can't even comprehend, plus polarized light. Polarized lenses are great for cutting glare when you're driving. I always wear them when I'm on patrol. \n\nBut anyway, no, I really don't know about the two weeks without being depressed. I just haven't paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a stretch of at least two weeks when you were hearing voices or seeing shadows, but your mood was otherwise normal?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't we just... I thought I just answered this? Two weeks, voices, mood... I really don't know how to break it down like that. It's not like I keep a calendar where I put a little smiley face on the days I feel normal and a ghost sticker on the days I see the shadows. Calendars are weird anyway. The Gregorian calendar is what we use, but it's totally arbitrary. Pope Gregory XIII introduced it in 1582 because the Julian calendar was drifting out of sync with the solar year. They actually had to skip ten days in October that year just to catch up. Imagine going to sleep on October 4th and waking up on October 15th. Time is just a construct, though. Einstein proved that with relativity. Time moves slower the faster you go, or the closer you are to a massive object. Like a black hole. The gravity is so intense not even light can escape. Light travels at 186,000 miles per second, which is the universal speed limit. Nothing can go faster than that, unless you're talking about quantum entanglement. Spooky action at a distance, Einstein called it. Two particles can be connected across the universe, and if you change one, the other changes instantly. It completely breaks the rules of classical physics. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know about the two weeks. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a full week or more when you were hearing voices or seeing shadows, but you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Are you... are you just asking me the same thing over and over? A week, two weeks, mood, shadows... I don't... I don't know. I really don't. It's just all mixed together. Mixed together like concrete. You know, the Romans invented concrete, and theirs was actually better than ours. They mixed volcanic ash with seawater, and it made this structure that actually gets stronger over time. That's why the Pantheon is still standing after two thousand years. The dome is incredible, it has that hole in the top, the oculus. It lets the rain in, but they built a drainage system right into the marble floor. Marble is metamorphic rock, right? It starts out as limestone and then heat and pressure change it. Pressure changes everything. Carbon turns into diamonds under enough pressure. Diamonds are supposed to be rare, but they're really not. The De Beers cartel just hoarded them to control the supply and artificially inflate the price. It's brilliant marketing, honestly. \"A diamond is forever.\" They convinced the whole world you need one to get married. Marriage is... well, it's a contract, basically. A legal binding. \n\nBut no, I... I don't have an answer about the week. I just don't know how to separate the shadows from how I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices or shadows ever continued for at least two weeks when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Are you messing with me? You literally just asked me this exact same thing. Two weeks, shadows, mood... I don't have a different answer for you. I don't. It feels like you're running an interrogation. Have you ever administered a polygraph? I had to sit for one years ago for a security clearance. They're completely inadmissible in court, you know. They don't actually detect lies, they just measure your galvanic skin response and your blood pressure. Blood pressure is measured in millimeters of mercury. Mercury is fascinating because it's a metal but it's completely liquid at room temperature. It used to be called quicksilver. Alchemists thought it was the first matter, like the base of all other metals. They spent centuries trying to turn lead into gold. Lead is super dense. They use it in those heavy aprons at the dentist when they take X-rays to block the radiation. Wilhelm Roentgen discovered X-rays by accident back in the 1890s. He took an X-ray of his wife's hand, and you can clearly see her wedding ring in the picture. When she saw the skeleton of her hand, she said she had seen her own death. People were terrified of X-rays at first, they thought it was black magic. \n\nBut look, doc, I can't give you the answer you're fishing for. I just don't track the shadows against my mood for fourteen days like that. I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices or shadows been present for most of the time while you’ve been depressed?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I... I don't know. It’s not like I have a stopwatch running when I feel down versus when the shadows show up. They just happen. It’s hard to tell what’s what when everything just feels dark, you know? Speaking of dark, working the night shift really messes with your perception of it. I’ve done night security for years, and you just sit there staring at those monitor screens. They use infrared cameras for the perimeter. Infrared is just below the visible light spectrum. Humans can't see it, but snakes can. Pit vipers have these little organs on their faces that let them sense infrared radiation, so they're basically seeing body heat. It’s how they hunt in the pitch black. Hunting is all about patience, really. My grandfather used to take me deer hunting up in the mountains when I was a kid. He had this old Winchester rifle, lever-action. The lever-action was iconic in the Wild West. They called the Winchester the gun that won the west. Which is a whole marketing myth anyway, kind of like the diamond thing I was talking about earlier. Most cowboys couldn't even afford them. They carried cheap revolvers. Samuel Colt actually got the idea for the revolving cylinder from watching a ship's wheel on a voyage to Calcutta when he was a teenager. \n\nBut yeah, the depression and the voices... I couldn't tell you if they line up most of the time. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least four days when you felt unusually irritable or upbeat and had much more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Four days of being upbeat or irritable? Um, no, I... I don't think I've ever tracked a four-day stretch like that. Energy is a weird thing, anyway. Energy can't be created or destroyed, right? That's the first law of thermodynamics. It just changes forms. Like kinetic energy turning into thermal energy when you hit the brakes on a car. The brake pads clamp down on the rotors, and the friction generates a massive amount of heat. That's why high-performance sports cars use carbon-ceramic brakes instead of steel. They can handle the extreme temperatures without warping. Carbon is so versatile. I mean, we were talking about diamonds earlier, but it's also in graphite, which is what pencil lead is made of. It's crazy that the same element can be the hardest substance on earth and also soft enough to write on paper just because of how the atoms are arranged. Atoms are mostly empty space, which is hard to wrap your head around. If you took all the empty space out of the atoms of every human on earth, the entire human race would fit into the volume of a sugar cube. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't really noticed any four-day periods of having way too much energy or being super irritable. I just don't have an answer for that."
